					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Head of the Catholic Church from 1492 to 1503</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Rodrigo de Borja" redirects here. Alexander</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Alexander VI</b><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;Note 2&#93;</a></sup> (1431&#160;– 18 August 1503), born <b>Rodrigo de Borja</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;Note 3&#93;</a></sup> was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> from 11 August 1492 until his death in 1503. Born into the prominent <a href="/wiki/House_of_Borgia" title="House of Borgia">Borgia family</a> in <a href="/wiki/X%C3%A0tiva" title="Xàtiva">Xàtiva</a> under the <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon" title="Crown of Aragon">Crown of Aragon</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>), Rodrigo studied law at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Bologna" title="University of Bologna">University of Bologna</a>. He was ordained <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> and made a <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">cardinal</a> in 1456 after the election of his uncle as <a href="/wiki/Pope_Callixtus_III" title="Pope Callixtus III">Pope Callixtus III</a>, and a year later he became <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Chancery" title="Apostolic Chancery">vice-chancellor</a> of the Catholic Church. He proceeded to serve in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Curia" title="Roman Curia">Curia</a> under the next four popes, acquiring significant influence and wealth in the process. In 1492, Rodrigo was elected pope, taking the name Alexander VI.
</p><p>Alexander's <a href="/wiki/Inter_caetera" title="Inter caetera">papal bulls</a> of 1493 confirmed or reconfirmed the rights of the Spanish crown in the <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a> following the finds of <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Columbus" title="Christopher Columbus">Christopher Columbus</a> in 1492. During the <a href="/wiki/Italian_wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian wars">second Italian war</a>, Alexander VI supported his son <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare Borgia</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Condottiero" title="Condottiero">condottiero</a> for the French king. The scope of his foreign policy was to gain the most advantageous terms for his family.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander is considered one of the most controversial of the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a> popes, partly because he acknowledged fathering several children by his mistresses. As a result, his Italianized <a href="/wiki/Valencian" class="mw-redirect" title="Valencian">Valencian</a> surname, <i>Borgia</i>, became a byword for <a href="/wiki/Libertinism" class="mw-redirect" title="Libertinism">libertinism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nepotism" title="Nepotism">nepotism</a>, which are traditionally considered as characterizing his pontificate. On the other hand, two of Alexander's successors, <a href="/wiki/Sixtus_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Sixtus V">Sixtus V</a> and <a href="/wiki/Urban_VIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Urban VIII">Urban VIII</a>, described him as one of the most outstanding popes since <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Saint Peter</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family">Birth and family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Birth and family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Rodrigo de Borja was born in 1431, in the town of <a href="/wiki/X%C3%A0tiva" title="Xàtiva">Xàtiva</a> near <a href="/wiki/Valencia" title="Valencia">Valencia</a>, one of the component realms of the <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon" title="Crown of Aragon">Crown of Aragon</a>, in what is now Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He was named for his paternal grandfather, <a href="/wiki/Rodrigo_Gil_de_Borja_y_Fennolet" title="Rodrigo Gil de Borja y Fennolet">Rodrigo Gil de Borja y Fennolet</a>. His parents were <a href="/wiki/Jofr%C3%A9_Llan%C3%A7ol_i_Escriv%C3%A0" title="Jofré Llançol i Escrivà">Jofré Llançol i Escrivà</a> (died bef. 24 March 1437) and his Aragonese wife and distant cousin Isabel de Borja y Cavanilles (died 19 October 1468), daughter of <a href="/wiki/Juan_Domingo_de_Borja_y_Doncel" class="mw-redirect" title="Juan Domingo de Borja y Doncel">Juan Domingo de Borja y Doncel</a>. He had a younger brother, <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Luis_de_Borja" title="Pedro Luis de Borja">Pedro</a>. His family name is written <i>Llançol</i> in <a href="/wiki/Valencian_language" title="Valencian language">Valencian</a> and <i>Lanzol</i> in <a href="/wiki/Castillian_Spanish" class="mw-redirect" title="Castillian Spanish">Castillian</a>. Rodrigo adopted his mother's family name of <a href="/wiki/Borgia" class="mw-redirect" title="Borgia">Borja</a> in 1455 following the elevation to the <a href="/wiki/Papacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Papacy">papacy</a> of maternal uncle Alonso de Borja (Italianized to Alfonso Borgia) as <a href="/wiki/Calixtus_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Calixtus III">Calixtus III</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> His cousin and Calixtus's nephew <a href="/wiki/Luis_de_Mil%C3%A0_y_de_Borja" title="Luis de Milà y de Borja">Luis de Milà y de Borja</a> became a cardinal.
</p><p>Gerard Noel writes that Rodrigo's father was Jofré de Borja y Escrivà, making Rodrigo a Borja from his mother and father's side.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> However, Cesare, Lucrezia and Jofre were known to be of Llançol paternal lineage. <a href="/wiki/G._J._Meyer" title="G. J. Meyer">G. J. Meyer</a> suggests that Rodrigo would have likely been uncle (from a shared female family member) to the children, and attributes the confusion to attempts to connect Rodrigo as the father of <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia,_2nd_Duke_of_Gandia" class="mw-redirect" title="Giovanni Borgia, 2nd Duke of Gandia">Giovanni</a> (Juan), <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lucrezia_Borgia" title="Lucrezia Borgia">Lucrezia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gioffre_Borgia" title="Gioffre Borgia">Gioffre</a> (Jofré in <a href="/wiki/Valencian_language" title="Valencian language">Valencian</a>), who were surnamed <i>Llançol i Borja</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Meyer_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meyer-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Career">Career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Rodrigo de Borja's career in the Church began in 1445 at the age of 14 when he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Sacristan" title="Sacristan">sacristan</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Valencia_Cathedral" title="Valencia Cathedral">Cathedral of Valencia</a> by his influential uncle, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Callixtus_III" title="Pope Callixtus III">Alfons Cardinal de Borja</a>, who had been appointed a <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">cardinal</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Eugene_IV" title="Pope Eugene IV">Pope Eugene IV</a> the previous year.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> In 1448, Borja became canon at the cathedrals of Valencia, Barcelona, and Segorbe. His uncle, Cardinal de Borja, persuaded <a href="/wiki/Pope_Nicholas_V" title="Pope Nicholas V">Pope Nicholas V</a> to allow young Borja to perform this role <i><a href="/wiki/In_absentia" title="In absentia">in absentia</a></i> and receive the associated income, so that Borja could travel to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> While in Rome, Rodrigo Borgia (as his surname was usually spelled in <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>) studied under Gaspare da Verona, a humanist tutor. He then studied law at <a href="/wiki/University_of_Bologna" title="University of Bologna">Bologna</a> where he graduated, not simply as <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">Doctor of Law</a>, but as "the most eminent and judicious jurisprudent."<sup id="cite_ref-deroo1_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-deroo1-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The election of his uncle, Alfons Cardinal de Borja, as <a href="/wiki/Pope_Callixtus_III" title="Pope Callixtus III">Pope Callixtus III</a> in 1455 enabled Borgia's appointments to other positions in the Church. These <a href="/wiki/Nepotism" title="Nepotism">nepotistic</a> appointments were characteristic of the era. Each pope during this period found himself surrounded by the servants and retainers of his predecessors who often owed their loyalty to the family of the pontiff who had appointed them.<sup id="cite_ref-Burkle_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Burkle-17">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>  In 1455, he inherited his uncle's post as bishop of Valencia, and Callixtus appointed him Dean of Santa Maria in Játiva. The following year, he was ordained <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> and created <a href="/wiki/Lay_cardinal" title="Lay cardinal">cardinal-deacon</a> of <a href="/wiki/San_Nicola_in_Carcere" title="San Nicola in Carcere">San Nicola in Carcere</a>. Rodrigo Borgia's appointment as cardinal only occurred after Callixtus III asked the cardinals in Rome to create three new positions in the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">College of Cardinals</a>, two for his nephews Rodrigo and Luis Juan de Milà, and one for the Prince Jaime of Portugal.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In 1457, Callixtus III assigned the young Cardinal de Borja (or Borgia in Italian) to go to Ancona as a Papal legate to quell a revolt. Borgia was successful in his mission, and his uncle rewarded him with his appointment as <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Chancery" title="Apostolic Chancery">vice-chancellor</a> of the Holy Roman Church.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The position of <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Chancery" title="Apostolic Chancery">vice-chancellor</a> was both incredibly powerful and lucrative, and Borgia held this post for 35 years until his own election to the papacy in 1492. At the end of 1457, Rodrigo Cardinal Borgia's elder brother, Pedro Luis Borgia, fell ill, so Rodrigo temporarily filled Pedro Luis' position as <a href="/wiki/Captain-general" class="mw-redirect" title="Captain-general">captain-general</a> of the papal army until he recovered.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In 1458, Cardinal Borgia's uncle and greatest benefactor, Pope Callixtus, died.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1458_papal_conclave" title="1458 papal conclave">papal election of 1458</a>, Rodrigo Borgia was too young to seek the papacy himself, so he sought to support a cardinal who would maintain him as vice-chancellor. Borgia was one of the deciding votes in the election of Cardinal Piccolomini as <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_II" title="Pope Pius II">Pope Pius II</a>, and the new pope rewarded Borgia not only with maintaining the chancellorship but also with a lucrative abbey benefice and another titular church.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In 1460, Pope Pius rebuked Cardinal Borgia for attending a private party which Pius had heard turned into an orgy. Borgia apologized for the incident but denied that there had been an orgy. Pope Pius forgave him, and the true events of the evening remain unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In 1462, Rodrigo Borgia had his first son, Pedro Luis, with an unknown mistress. He sent Pedro Luis to grow up in Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The following year, Borgia acceded to Pope Pius's call for cardinals to help fund a new crusade. Before embarking to lead the crusade personally, Pope Pius II fell ill and died, so Borgia would need to ensure the election of yet another ally to the papacy to maintain his position as vice-chancellor.
</p><p>On the first ballot, the <a href="/wiki/1464_papal_conclave" title="1464 papal conclave">conclave of 1464</a> elected Borgia's friend Pietro Barbo as <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_II" title="Pope Paul II">Pope Paul II</a>. Borgia was in high standing with the new pope and retained his positions, including that of vice-chancellor. Paul II reversed some of his predecessor's reforms that diminished the power of the chancellory. Following the election, Borgia fell ill of the plague but recovered. Borgia had two daughters, Isabella (*1467) and Girolama (*1469), with an unknown mistress. He openly acknowledged all three of his children.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Pope Paul II died suddenly in 1471.
</p><p>While Borgia had acquired the reputation and wealth to mount a bid for the papacy in <a href="/wiki/1471_papal_conclave" title="1471 papal conclave">this conclave</a>, there were only three non-Italians, making his election a near-impossibility. Consequently, Borgia continued his previous strategy of positioning himself as kingmaker. This time, Borgia gathered the votes to make Francesco della Rovere (the uncle of future Borgia rival <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Giuliano della Rovere</a>) <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_IV" title="Pope Sixtus IV">Pope Sixtus IV</a>. Della Rovere's appeal was that he was a pious and brilliant <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscan</a> monk who lacked many political connections in Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He seemed to be the perfect cardinal to reform the Church, and the perfect cardinal for Borgia to maintain his influence. Sixtus IV rewarded Borgia for his support by promoting him to cardinal-bishop and consecrating him as the <a href="/wiki/Cardinal-Bishop_of_Albano" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal-Bishop of Albano">Cardinal-Bishop of Albano</a>, requiring Borgia's ordination as a priest. Borgia also received a lucrative abbey from the pope and remained vice-chancellor.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> At the end of the year, the pope appointed Borgia to be the papal legate for Spain to negotiate a peace treaty between <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Castile" title="Crown of Castile">Castile</a> and <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon" title="Crown of Aragon">Aragon</a> and to solicit their support for another crusade. In 1472, Borgia was appointed to be the papal chamberlain until his departure to Spain. Borgia arrived in his native Aragon in the summer, reuniting with family and meeting with King Juan II and Prince Ferdinand. The pope gave Cardinal Borgia discretion over whether to give dispensation for <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">Ferdinand</a>'s marriage to his first cousin <a href="/wiki/Isabella_I_of_Castile" title="Isabella I of Castile">Isabella of Castile</a>, and Borgia decided in favour of approving the marriage. The couple named Borgia to be the godfather of their first son in recognition of this decision.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The marriage of Ferdinand and Isabella was critical in the <a href="/wiki/Habsburg_Spain" title="Habsburg Spain">unification of Castile and Aragon</a> into Spain. Borgia also negotiated peace between Castile and Aragon and an end to the civil wars in the latter Kingdom, gaining the favour of the future King Ferdinand who would go on to promote the interests of the Borgia family in Aragon.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Borgia returned to Rome the following year, narrowly surviving a storm that sunk a nearby galley that was carrying 200 men of the Borgia household. Back in Rome, Borgia began his affair with <a href="/wiki/Vannozza_dei_Cattanei" title="Vannozza dei Cattanei">Vannozza dei Cattenei</a> which would yield four children: <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare</a> in 1475, <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia,_2nd_Duke_of_Gand%C3%ADa" title="Giovanni Borgia, 2nd Duke of Gandía">Giovanni</a> in 1474 or 1476, <a href="/wiki/Lucrezia_Borgia" title="Lucrezia Borgia">Lucrezia</a> in 1480, and <a href="/wiki/Gioffre_Borgia" title="Gioffre Borgia">Gioffre</a> in 1482. In 1476, Pope Sixtus appointed Borgia to be the cardinal-bishop of Porto. In 1480, the pope legitimized Cesare as a favour to Cardinal Borgia, and in 1482, the pope began to appoint the seven-year-old to church positions, demonstrating Borgia's intention to use his influence to promote his children. Contemporaneously, Borgia continued to add to his list of benefices, becoming the wealthiest cardinal by 1483.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> He also become <a href="/wiki/Dean_of_the_College_of_Cardinals" title="Dean of the College of Cardinals">Dean of the College of Cardinals</a> in that year. In 1484, Pope Sixtus IV died, necessitating another election for Borgia to manipulate to his advantage.
</p><p>Borgia was wealthy and powerful enough to mount a bid, but he faced competition from <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Giuliano della Rovere</a>, the late pope's nephew. Della Rovere's faction had the advantage of being incredibly large as Sixtus had appointed many of the cardinals who would participate in the election. Borgia's attempts to gather enough votes included bribery and leveraging his close ties to Naples and Aragon. However, many of the Spanish cardinals were absent from the conclave and della Rovere's faction had an overwhelming advantage. Della Rovere chose to promote Cardinal Cibo as his preferred candidate, and Cibo wrote to the Borgia faction wanting to strike a deal. Once again, Borgia played kingmaker and conceded to Cardinal Cibo who became <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_VIII" title="Pope Innocent VIII">Pope Innocent VIII</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Again, Borgia retained his position of vice-chancellor, successfully holding this position over the course of five papacies and four elections.
</p><p>In 1485, Pope Innocent VIII nominated Borgia to become the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Seville" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Seville">Archbishop of Seville</a>, a position that <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">King Ferdinand II</a> wanted for his own son. In response, Ferdinand angrily seized the Borgia estates in Aragon and imprisoned Borgia's son Pedro Luis. However, Borgia healed the relationship by turning down this appointment. Pope Innocent, at the urging of his close ally Giuliano della Rovere, decided to declare war against Naples, but Milan, Florence, and Aragon chose to support Naples over the pope. Borgia led the opposition within the College of Cardinals to this war, and King Ferdinand rewarded Borgia by making his son Pedro Luis the <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Gand%C3%ADa" title="Duke of Gandía">Duke of Gandia</a> and arranging a marriage between his cousin Maria Enriquez and the new duke. Now, the Borgia family was directly tied to the royal families of Spain and Naples. While Borgia gained the favour of Spain, he stood opposed to the pope and the della Rovere family. As a part of his war opposition, Borgia sought to obstruct an alliance negotiation between the papacy and France. These negotiations were unsuccessful and in July 1486, the pope capitulated and ended the war.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In 1488, Borgia's son Pedro Luis died, and Juan Borgia became the new duke of Gandia. In the following year, Borgia hosted the wedding ceremony between <a href="/wiki/Orsino_Orsini" title="Orsino Orsini">Orsino Orsini</a> and <a href="/wiki/Giulia_Farnese" title="Giulia Farnese">Giulia Farnese</a>, and within a few months, Farnese had become Borgia's new mistress. She was 15, and he was 58.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Borgia continued to acquire new benefices with their large streams of income, including the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Majorca" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Majorca">bishopric of Majorca</a> and Eger in Hungary. In 1492, Pope Innocent VIII died. Since Borgia was 61, this was likely his last chance to become pope.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Appearance_and_personality">Appearance and personality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Appearance and personality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Peter de Roo gives a flattering summary of contemporary descriptions of Alexander, relating him to have been "of a medium complexion, with dark eyes and 
slightly full lips, of robust health"; in later life, he reports that "his aspect [was declared] to be venerable and far more 
august than an ordinary human appearance", and that he was "so familiar with <a href="/wiki/Holy_Writ" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Writ">Holy Writ</a>, that his speeches were fairly sparkling with well-chosen texts of the Sacred Books".<sup id="cite_ref-deroo1_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-deroo1-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg/220px-Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="406" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg/330px-Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg/440px-Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1615" data-file-height="2980" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Plaque_Borja_Valencia.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Plaque outside the Archbishop's Palace, <a href="/wiki/Valencia" title="Valencia">Valencia</a> . Translation: Alexander VI, 9 July 1492, Pope Innocent VIII, at the request of Cardinal Borja and the Catholic Monarchs, raised the Valencian See to the rank of metropolitan, making Rodrigo of Borja the first Archbishop of Valencia 1492–1503.</div></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Archbishop_of_Valencia">Archbishop of Valencia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Archbishop of Valencia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>When his uncle Alonso de Borja (<a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Valencia_in_Spain" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Valencia in Spain">bishop of Valencia</a>) was elected Pope Callixtus III,
he "inherited" the post of bishop of Valencia. Sixteen days before the death of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_VIII" title="Pope Innocent VIII">Pope Innocent VIII</a>, he proposed Valencia as a <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_bishop" title="Metropolitan bishop">metropolitan</a> <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_see" title="Episcopal see">see</a> and became the first archbishop of Valencia. When Rodrigo de Borgia was elected pope as Alexander VI following the death of Innocent VIII, his son Cesare Borgia "inherited" the post as second archbishop of Valencia. The third and the fourth archbishops of Valencia were <a href="/wiki/Juan_de_Borja_Lanzol_de_Roman%C3%AD,_el_menor" title="Juan de Borja Lanzol de Romaní, el menor">Juan de Borja</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Luis_de_Borja" title="Pedro Luis de Borja">Pedro Luis de Borja</a>, grand-nephews of Alexander VI.<sup id="cite_ref-moroni_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moroni-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Election">Election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1492" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1492">Papal conclave, 1492</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tleft"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:164px;max-width:164px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:162px;max-width:162px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Pope_Alexander_VI_Borgia_-_Castel_Sant%27Angelo,_Rome.JPG" class="image"><img alt="Coat of arms of Alexander VI – Castel Sant&#39;Angelo, Rome" decoding="async" width="160" height="189" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Coat_of_Arms_of_Pope_Alexander_VI_Borgia_-_Castel_Sant%27Angelo%2C_Rome.JPG/240px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Pope_Alexander_VI_Borgia_-_Castel_Sant%27Angelo%2C_Rome.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Coat_of_Arms_of_Pope_Alexander_VI_Borgia_-_Castel_Sant%27Angelo%2C_Rome.JPG/320px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Pope_Alexander_VI_Borgia_-_Castel_Sant%27Angelo%2C_Rome.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2070" data-file-height="2439" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">Coat of arms of Alexander VI – Castel Sant'Angelo, Rome</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>There was change in the constitution of the College of Cardinals during the course of the 15th century, especially under Sixtus IV and Innocent VIII. Of the 27 cardinals alive in the closing months of the reign of Innocent VIII no fewer than 10 were <a href="/wiki/Cardinal-nephew" title="Cardinal-nephew">cardinal-nephews</a>, eight were crown nominees, four were Roman nobles and one other had been given the cardinalate in recompense for his family's service to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>; only four were able career churchmen.<sup id="cite_ref-Burkle_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Burkle-17">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the death of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_VIII" title="Pope Innocent VIII">Pope Innocent VIII</a> on 25 July 1492, the three likely candidates for the <a href="/wiki/Papacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Papacy">Papacy</a> were the 61-year-old Borgia,<sup id="cite_ref-Burkle_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Burkle-17">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> seen as an independent candidate, <a href="/wiki/Ascanio_Sforza" title="Ascanio Sforza">Ascanio Sforza</a> for the Milanese, and <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_II" title="Pope Julius II">Giuliano della Rovere</a>, seen as a pro-French candidate.
It was rumoured but not substantiated that Borgia succeeded in buying the largest number of votes and Sforza, in particular, was bribed with four mule-loads of silver.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Mallett shows that Borgia was in the lead from the start and that the rumours of bribery began after the election with the distribution of benefices; Sforza and della Rovere were just as willing and able to bribe as anyone else.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The benefices and offices granted to Sforza, moreover, would be worth considerably more than four mule-loads of silver. <a href="/wiki/Johann_Burchard" title="Johann Burchard">Johann Burchard</a>, the conclave's master of ceremonies and a leading figure of the papal household under several popes, recorded in his diary that the <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1492" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1492">1492 conclave</a> was a particularly expensive campaign. Della Rovere was bankrolled to the cost of 200,000 gold <a href="/wiki/Ducat" title="Ducat">ducats</a> by <a href="/wiki/Charles_VIII_of_France" title="Charles VIII of France">King Charles VIII of France</a>, with another 100,000 supplied by the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa" title="Republic of Genoa">Republic of Genoa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Burchard_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Burchard-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The leading candidates in the first ballot were Oliviero Carafa of Sforza's party with nine votes, and Giovanni Michiel and Jorge Costa, both of della Rovere's party with seven votes each. Borgia himself gathered seven votes. However,  Borgia convinced Sforza to join with his camp through the promise of being appointed vice-chancellor as well as bribes that included benefices and perhaps four mule-loads of silver. With Sforza now canvassing for votes, Borgia's election was assured. Borgia was elected on 11 August 1492 and assumed the name of Alexander VI (due to confusion about the status of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Alexander V">Pope Alexander V</a>, elected by the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Pisa" title="Council of Pisa">Council of Pisa</a>). Many inhabitants of Rome were happy with their new pope because he was a generous and competent administrator who had served for decades as vice-chancellor.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years_in_office">Early years in office</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Early years in office">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_(FindID_53537).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_%28FindID_53537%29.jpg/220px-Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_%28FindID_53537%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="113" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_%28FindID_53537%29.jpg/330px-Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_%28FindID_53537%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_%28FindID_53537%29.jpg/440px-Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_%28FindID_53537%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1761" data-file-height="902" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Lead_papal_bulla_of_Alexander_VI_(FindID_53537).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Papal_bulla" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal bulla">Papal bulla</a> of Alexander VI</div></div></div>
<p>In contrast to the preceding pontificate, Pope Alexander VI adhered initially to strict administration of justice and orderly government. Before long, though, he began endowing his relatives at the Church's and at his neighbours' expense. <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare Borgia</a>, his son, while a youth of seventeen and a student at <a href="/wiki/Pisa" title="Pisa">Pisa</a>, was made <a href="/wiki/Archbishop" title="Archbishop">Archbishop</a> of <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Valencia" title="Province of Valencia">Valencia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia,_2nd_Duke_of_Gandia" class="mw-redirect" title="Giovanni Borgia, 2nd Duke of Gandia">Giovanni Borgia</a> inherited the Spanish Dukedom of <a href="/wiki/Gandia" title="Gandia">Gandia</a>, the Borgias' ancestral home in Spain. For the Duke of Gandia and for <a href="/wiki/Gioffre_Borgia" title="Gioffre Borgia">Gioffre</a>, also known as Goffredo, the Pope proposed to carve fiefs out of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Naples" title="Kingdom of Naples">Kingdom of Naples</a>. Among the fiefs destined for the duke of Gandia were <a href="/wiki/Cerveteri" title="Cerveteri">Cerveteri</a> and <a href="/wiki/Anguillara_Sabazia" title="Anguillara Sabazia">Anguillara</a>, lately acquired by <a href="/wiki/Virginio_Orsini" title="Virginio Orsini">Virginio Orsini</a>, head of that powerful house. This policy brought <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Naples" title="Ferdinand I of Naples">Ferdinand I of Naples</a> into conflict with Alexander, as well as with Cardinal della Rovere, whose candidature for the papacy had been backed by Ferdinand. Della Rovere fortified himself in his <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Ostia" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Ostia">bishopric of Ostia</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Tiber" title="Tiber">Tiber</a>'s mouth as Alexander formed a league against Naples (25 April 1493) and prepared for war.<sup id="cite_ref-Villari_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Villari-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ferdinand allied himself with <a href="/wiki/Florence" title="Florence">Florence</a>, <a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a>. He also appealed to Spain for help, but Spain was eager to be on good terms with the papacy to obtain the title to the recently discovered <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a>. Alexander, in the bull <i><a href="/wiki/Inter_caetera" title="Inter caetera">Inter caetera</a></i> on 4 May 1493, divided the title between Spain and Portugal along a demarcation line. This became the basis of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Tordesillas" title="Treaty of Tordesillas">Treaty of Tordesillas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="French_involvement">French involvement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: French involvement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Italy_1494_AD.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Italy_1494_AD.png/220px-Italy_1494_AD.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="315" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Italy_1494_AD.png/330px-Italy_1494_AD.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Italy_1494_AD.png/440px-Italy_1494_AD.png 2x" data-file-width="1261" data-file-height="1805" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Italy_1494_AD.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Italy 1494</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Italian_War_of_1494%E2%80%931498" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian War of 1494–1498">Italian War of 1494–1498</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2014</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Pope Alexander VI made many alliances to secure his position. He sought help from <a href="/wiki/Charles_VIII_of_France" title="Charles VIII of France">Charles VIII of France</a> (1483–1498), who was allied to <a href="/wiki/Ludovico_Sforza" title="Ludovico Sforza">Ludovico "il Moro" Sforza</a> (the Moor, so-called because of his swarthy complexion), the <i>de facto</i> Duke of Milan, who needed French support to legitimise his rule. As King <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Naples" title="Ferdinand I of Naples">Ferdinand I of Naples</a> was threatening to come to the aid of the rightful duke <a href="/wiki/Gian_Galeazzo_Sforza" title="Gian Galeazzo Sforza">Gian Galeazzo Sforza</a>, the husband of his granddaughter <a href="/wiki/Isabella_of_Aragon,_Duchess_of_Milan" title="Isabella of Aragon, Duchess of Milan">Isabella</a>, Alexander encouraged the French king in his plan for the conquest of Naples.
</p><p>But Alexander, always ready to seize opportunities to aggrandize his family, then adopted a double policy. Through the intervention of the Spanish ambassador he made peace with Naples in July 1493 and cemented the peace by a marriage between his son Gioffre and <a href="/wiki/Sancha_of_Aragon" title="Sancha of Aragon">Doña Sancha</a>, another granddaughter of Ferdinand I.<sup id="cite_ref-Burchard_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Burchard-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In order to dominate the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">College of Cardinals</a> more completely, Alexander, in a move that created much scandal, created 12 new cardinals. Among the new cardinals was his own son Cesare, then only 18 years old. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_III" title="Pope Paul III">Alessandro Farnese</a> (later Pope Paul III), the brother of one of the Pope's mistresses, <a href="/wiki/Giulia_Farnese" title="Giulia Farnese">Giulia Farnese</a>, was also among the newly created cardinals.
</p><p>On 25 January 1494, Ferdinand I died and was succeeded by his son <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_II_of_Naples" title="Alfonso II of Naples">Alfonso II</a> (1494–1495).<sup id="cite_ref-Burchard_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Burchard-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Charles_VIII_of_France" title="Charles VIII of France">Charles VIII of France</a> now advanced formal claims on the Kingdom of Naples. Alexander authorised him to pass through Rome, ostensibly on a <a href="/wiki/Crusade" class="mw-redirect" title="Crusade">crusade</a> against the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>, without mentioning Naples. But when the French invasion became a reality Pope Alexander VI became alarmed, recognised Alfonso II as king of Naples, and concluded an alliance with him in exchange for various fiefs for his sons (July 1494). A military response to the French threat was set in motion: a Neapolitan army was to advance through <a href="/wiki/Romagna" title="Romagna">Romagna</a> and attack Milan, while the fleet was to seize <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Genoa</a>. Both expeditions were badly conducted and failed, and on 8 September Charles VIII crossed the <a href="/wiki/Alps" title="Alps">Alps</a> and joined Ludovico il Moro at Milan. The Papal States were in turmoil, and the powerful <a href="/wiki/Colonna" class="mw-redirect" title="Colonna">Colonna</a> faction seized <a href="/wiki/Ostia_(district)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ostia (district)">Ostia</a> in the name of France. Charles VIII rapidly advanced southward, and after a short stay in Florence, set out for Rome (November 1494).
</p><p>Alexander appealed to <a href="/wiki/Ascanio_Sforza" title="Ascanio Sforza">Ascanio Sforza</a> and even to the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman</a> <a href="/wiki/Sultan" title="Sultan">Sultan</a> <a href="/wiki/Bayazid_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Bayazid II">Bayazid II</a> for help. He tried to collect troops and put Rome in a state of defence, but his position was precarious. When the Orsini offered to admit the French to their castles, Alexander had no choice but to come to terms with Charles. On 31 December, Charles VIII entered Rome with his troops, the cardinals of the French faction, and Giuliano della Rovere. Alexander now feared that Charles might depose him for <a href="/wiki/Simony" title="Simony">simony</a>, and that the king would summon a council to nominate a new pope. Alexander was able to win over the <a href="/wiki/Guillaume_Bri%C3%A7onnet_(Cardinal)" class="mw-redirect" title="Guillaume Briçonnet (Cardinal)">bishop of Saint-Malo</a>, who had much influence over the king, by making him a cardinal. Alexander agreed to send Cesare as legate to Naples with the French army; to deliver <a href="/wiki/Cem_Sultan" title="Cem Sultan">Cem Sultan</a>, held as a hostage, to Charles VIII, and to give Charles <a href="/wiki/Civitavecchia" title="Civitavecchia">Civitavecchia</a> (16 January 1495). On 28 January Charles VIII departed for Naples with Cem and Cesare, but the latter slipped away to <a href="/wiki/Spoleto" title="Spoleto">Spoleto</a>. Neapolitan resistance collapsed, and Alfonso II fled and abdicated in favour of his son <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Naples" title="Ferdinand II of Naples">Ferdinand II</a>. Ferdinand was abandoned by all and also had to escape, and the Kingdom of Naples was conquered with surprising ease.<sup id="cite_ref-CE1913_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE1913-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="French_in_retreat">French in retreat</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: French in retreat">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2014</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>A reaction against Charles VIII soon set in, for all the European powers were alarmed at his success. On 31 March 1495 the <a href="/wiki/Holy_League_(1495)" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy League (1495)">Holy League</a> was formed between the pope, the emperor, <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a>, Ludovico il Moro and <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">Ferdinand of Spain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The League was ostensibly formed against the Turks, but in reality it was made to expel the French from Italy. Charles VIII had himself crowned King of <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Naples" title="Kingdom of Naples">Naples</a> on 12 May, but a few days later began his retreat northward. He met the League at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Fornovo" title="Battle of Fornovo">Fornovo</a> and cut his way through them and was back in France by November. Ferdinand II was reinstated at <a href="/wiki/Naples" title="Naples">Naples</a> soon afterwards, with Spanish help. The expedition, if it produced no material results, demonstrated the foolishness of the so-called "politics of equilibrium", the Medicean doctrine of preventing one of the Italian principates from overwhelming the rest and uniting them under its hegemony.
</p><p>Charles VIII's belligerence in Italy had made it transparent that the "politics of equilibrium" did nothing but render the country unable to defend itself against a powerful invading force. Italy was shown to be very vulnerable to the predations of the powerful nation-states, France and Spain, that had forged themselves during the previous century. Alexander VI now followed the general tendency of all the princes of the day to crush the great feudatories and establish a centralized despotism. In this manner, he was able to take advantage of the defeat of the French in order to break the power of the Orsini. From that time on, Alexander was able to build himself an effective power base in the Papal States.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg/220px-Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg/330px-Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg/440px-Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1955" data-file-height="1537" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Castel_Sant%27Angelo_bild.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Castel_Sant%27Angelo" title="Castel Sant&#39;Angelo">Castel Sant'Angelo</a> in Rome</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Virginio_Orsini" title="Virginio Orsini">Virginio Orsini</a>, who had been captured by the Spanish, died a prisoner at Naples, and the Pope confiscated his property. The rest of the Orsini clan still held out, defeating the papal troops sent against them under <a href="/wiki/Guidobaldo_da_Montefeltro" title="Guidobaldo da Montefeltro">Guidobaldo da Montefeltro</a>, Duke of <a href="/wiki/Urbino" title="Urbino">Urbino</a> and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia_(1474)" class="mw-redirect" title="Giovanni Borgia (1474)">Giovanni Borgia</a>, Duke of Gandia, at <a href="/wiki/Soriano_nel_Cimino" title="Soriano nel Cimino">Soriano</a> (January 1497). Peace was made through Venetian mediation, the Orsini paying 50,000 ducats in exchange for their confiscated lands; the Duke of Urbino, whom they had captured, was left by the pope to pay his own ransom. The Orsini remained very powerful, and Pope Alexander VI could count on none but his 3,000 Spanish troops. His only success had been the capture of Ostia and the submission of the Francophile cardinals Colonna and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Battista_Savelli" title="Giovanni Battista Savelli">Savelli</a>.
</p><p>Then occurred a major domestic tragedy for the house of Borgia. On 14 June, his son the <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia,_2nd_Duke_of_Gand%C3%ADa" title="Giovanni Borgia, 2nd Duke of Gandía">Duke of Gandia</a>, who was lately created Duke of <a href="/wiki/Benevento" title="Benevento">Benevento</a> and had a questionable lifestyle, disappeared; the next day, his corpse was found in the Tiber.<sup id="cite_ref-Villari_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Villari-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Alexander, overwhelmed with grief, shut himself up in <a href="/wiki/Castel_Sant%27Angelo" title="Castel Sant&#39;Angelo">Castel Sant'Angelo</a>. He declared that henceforth the moral reform of the Church would be the sole object of his life. Every effort was made to discover the assassin. No conclusive explanation was ever reached,<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> and it may be that the crime was simply as a result of one of the Duke's sexual liaisons.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Crime">Crime</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Crime">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-Disputed plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-disputed" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><img alt="System-search.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/System-search.svg/45px-System-search.svg.png" decoding="async" width="45" height="45" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/System-search.svg/68px-System-search.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/System-search.svg/90px-System-search.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="48" data-file-height="48" /></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section's <b>factual accuracy is <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute" title="Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute">disputed</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Relevant discussion may be found on <a href="/wiki/Talk:Pope_Alexander_VI#Dispute" title="Talk:Pope Alexander VI">Talk:Pope Alexander VI</a>. Please help to ensure that disputed statements are <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources">reliably sourced</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2022</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>There is no evidence that the Borgias resorted to poisoning, judicial murder, or extortion to fund their schemes and the defense of the Papal States. The only contemporary accusations of poisoning were from some of their servants, extracted under torture by Alexander's bitter enemy Della Rovere, who succeeded him as Pope <a href="/wiki/Julius_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Julius II">Julius II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Savonarola">Savonarola</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Savonarola">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg/170px-Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg/255px-Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg/340px-Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg 2x" data-file-width="360" data-file-height="480" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Girolamo_Savonarola.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Savonarola" title="Girolamo Savonarola">Girolamo Savonarola</a></div></div></div>
<p>The debased state of the curia was a major scandal. Opponents, such as the powerful Florentine friar <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Savonarola" title="Girolamo Savonarola">Girolamo Savonarola</a>, launched invectives against papal corruption and appealed for a general council to confront the papal abuses. Alexander is reported to have been reduced to laughter when Savonarola's denunciations were related to him. Nevertheless, he appointed <a href="/wiki/Sebastian_Maggi" title="Sebastian Maggi">Sebastian Maggi</a> to investigate the friar, and he responded on 16 October 1495:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>We are displeased at the disturbed state of affairs in Florence, the more so in that it owes its origin to your preaching. For you predict the future and publicly declare that you do so by the inspiration of the Holy Spirit when you should be reprehending vice and praising virtue ... Prophecies like these should not be made when your charge is to forward peace and concord. Moreover, these are not the time for such teachings, calculated as they are to produce discord even in times of peace let alone in times of trouble. ... Since, however, we have been most happy to learn from certain cardinals and from your letter that you are ready to submit yourself to the reproofs of the Church, as becomes a Christian and a religious, we are beginning to think that what you have done has not been done with an evil motive, but from a certain simple-mindedness and a zeal, however misguided, for the Lord's vineyard. Our duty, however, prescribes that we order you, under holy obedience, to cease from public and private preaching until you are able to come to our presence, not under armed escort as is your present habit, but safely, quietly and modestly as becomes a religious, or until we make different arrangements. If you obey, as we hope you will, we for the time being suspend the operation of our former Brief so that you may live in peace in accordance with the dictates of your conscience.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The hostility of Savonarola seems to have been political rather than personal, and the friar sent a letter of condolence to the pope on the death of the Duke of Gandia; "Faith, most Holy Father, is the one and true source of peace and consolation... Faith alone brings consolation from a far-off country."<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> But eventually the Florentines tired of the friar's moralising and the Florentine government condemned the reformer to death, executing him on 23 May 1498.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Familial_aggrandizement">Familial aggrandizement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Familial aggrandizement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The prominent Italian families looked down on the Spanish Borgia family, and they resented their power, which they sought for themselves. This is, at least partially, why both Pope Callixtus III and Pope Alexander VI gave powers to family members whom they could trust.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;Note 4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22,_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22%2C_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22%2C_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22%2C_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22%2C_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22%2C_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22%2C_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6520" data-file-height="5220" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexander_-_Bolla_%22Desiderando_nui%22,_dopo_il_18_settembre_1499_-_2951587.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Papal bull <i>Desiderando nui</i>, 1499</div></div></div>
<p>In these circumstances, Alexander, feeling more than ever that he could rely only on his own kin, turned his thoughts to further family aggrandizement. He had annulled Lucrezia's marriage to <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Sforza" title="Giovanni Sforza">Giovanni Sforza</a>, who had responded to the suggestion that he was impotent with the unsubstantiated counter-claim that Alexander and Cesare indulged in incestuous relations with Lucrezia, in 1497.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;Note 5&#93;</a></sup> Unable to arrange a union between Cesare and the daughter of King <a href="/wiki/Frederick_IV_of_Naples" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick IV of Naples">Frederick IV of Naples</a> (who had succeeded Ferdinand II the previous year), he induced Frederick by threats to agree to a marriage between the Duke of <a href="/wiki/Bisceglie" title="Bisceglie">Bisceglie</a>, a natural son of Alfonso II, and Lucrezia. Alexander and the new French king <a href="/wiki/Louis_XII" title="Louis XII">Louis XII</a> entered a secret agreement; in exchange for a bull of divorce between the king and <a href="/wiki/Joan_of_France,_Duchess_of_Berry" title="Joan of France, Duchess of Berry">Joan of France</a> (so he could marry <a href="/wiki/Anne_of_Brittany" title="Anne of Brittany">Anne of Brittany</a>) and making <a href="/wiki/Georges_d%27Amboise" title="Georges d&#39;Amboise">Georges d'Amboise</a> (the king's chief advisor) the cardinal of <a href="/wiki/Rouen" title="Rouen">Rouen</a>, Cesare was given the duchy of <a href="/wiki/Valentinois" class="mw-redirect" title="Valentinois">Valentinois</a> (chosen because it was consistent with his nickname, Valentino), military assistance to help him subjugate the feudal princelings of papal Romagna,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> and a princess bride, <a href="/wiki/Charlotte_of_Albret" title="Charlotte of Albret">Charlotte of Albret</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Navarre" title="Kingdom of Navarre">Kingdom of Navarre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander hoped that Louis XII's help would be more profitable to his house than that of Charles VIII had been. In spite of the remonstrances of Spain and of the Sforza, he allied himself with France in January 1499 and was joined by Venice. By autumn Louis XII was in Italy expelling Lodovico Sforza from Milan. With French success seemingly assured, the Pope determined to deal drastically with Romagna, which although nominally under papal rule was divided into a number of practically independent lordships on which Venice, Milan, and Florence cast hungry eyes. Cesare, empowered by the support of the French, began to attack the turbulent cities one by one in his capacity as nominated <i><a href="/wiki/Gonfalone_of_the_Church" title="Gonfalone of the Church">gonfaloniere</a></i> (standard bearer) of the church. But the expulsion of the French from Milan and the return of Lodovico Sforza interrupted his conquests, and he returned to Rome early in 1500.
</p>
<h2><span id="The_Jubilee_.281500.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Jubilee_(1500)">The Jubilee (1500)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: The Jubilee (1500)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Jubilee_(Christianity)" class="mw-redirect" title="Jubilee (Christianity)">Jubilee year</a> 1500, Alexander ushered in the custom of opening a <a href="/wiki/Holy_door" title="Holy door">holy door</a> on Christmas Eve and closing it on Christmas Day the following year. After consulting with his Master of Ceremonies, <a href="/wiki/Johann_Burchard" title="Johann Burchard">Johann Burchard</a>, Pope Alexander VI opened the first holy door in <a href="/wiki/Old_St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="Old St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a> on Christmas Eve 1499, and papal representatives opened the doors in the other three patriarchal basilicas. For this, Pope Alexander had a new opening created in the <i><a href="/wiki/Portico" title="Portico">portico</a></i> of St. Peter's and commissioned a marble door.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;Note 6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;Note 7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander was carried in the <i><a href="/wiki/Sedia_gestatoria" title="Sedia gestatoria">sedia gestatoria</a></i> to St. Peter's. He and his assistants, bearing candles, processed to the holy door, as the choir chanted Psalm 118:19–20.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The pope knocked on the door three times, workers moved it from the inside, and everyone then crossed the threshold to enter into a period of penance and reconciliation. Thus, Pope Alexander formalized the rite and began a longstanding tradition that is still in practice. Similar ceremonies were held at the other three basilicas.<sup id="cite_ref-holydoors_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-holydoors-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander instituted a special rite for the closing of a holy door, as well. On the <a href="/wiki/Epiphany_(holiday)" title="Epiphany (holiday)">Feast of the Epiphany</a> in 1501, two cardinals began to seal the holy door with two bricks, one silver and one gold. <i>Sampietrini</i> (basilica workers)<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;Note 8&#93;</a></sup> completed the seal, placing specially-minted coins and medals inside the wall.<sup id="cite_ref-holydoors_52-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-holydoors-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Slavery">Slavery</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Slavery">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>While the explorers of Spain imposed a form of slavery called "<a href="/wiki/Encomienda" title="Encomienda">encomienda</a>" on the indigenous peoples they met in the New World,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> some popes had spoken out against the practice of slavery. In 1435, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Eugene_IV" title="Pope Eugene IV">Pope Eugene IV</a> had issued an attack on slavery in the <a href="/wiki/Canary_Islands" title="Canary Islands">Canary Islands</a> in his papal bull <i><a href="/wiki/Sicut_dudum" title="Sicut dudum">Sicut dudum</a></i>, which included the excommunication of all those who engaged in the slave trade with native chiefs there. A form of <a href="/wiki/Indentured_servitude" title="Indentured servitude">indentured servitude</a> was allowed, being similar to a peasant's duty to his <a href="/wiki/Homage_(feudal)" title="Homage (feudal)">liege lord</a> in Europe.
</p><p>In the wake of <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Columbus" title="Christopher Columbus">Columbus</a>'s landing in the New World, Pope Alexander was asked by the Spanish monarchy to confirm their ownership of these newly found lands.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The bulls issued by Pope Alexander VI: <i><a href="/wiki/Eximiae_devotionis" title="Eximiae devotionis">Eximiae devotionis</a></i> (3 May 1493), <i><a href="/wiki/Inter_caetera" title="Inter caetera">Inter caetera</a></i> (4 May 1493) and <i><a href="/wiki/Dudum_siquidem" title="Dudum siquidem">Dudum siquidem</a></i> (23 September 1493), granted rights to Spain with respect to the newly discovered lands in the Americas similar to those <a href="/wiki/Pope_Nicholas_V" title="Pope Nicholas V">Pope Nicholas V</a> had previously conferred on Portugal with the bulls <i><a href="/wiki/Romanus_Pontifex" title="Romanus Pontifex">Romanus Pontifex</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Dum_Diversas" title="Dum Diversas">Dum Diversas</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Minnich_281_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Minnich_281-62">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Morales Padron (1979) concludes that these bulls gave power to enslave the natives.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Minnich (2010) asserts that this "slave trade" was permitted to facilitate conversions to Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-Minnich_281_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Minnich_281-62">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Other historians and Vatican scholars strongly disagree with these accusations and assert that Alexander never gave his approval to the practice of slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Other later popes, such as <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_III" title="Pope Paul III">Pope Paul III</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/Sublimis_Deus" title="Sublimis Deus">Sublimis Deus</a></i> (1537), <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XIV" title="Pope Benedict XIV">Pope Benedict XIV</a> in <i>Immensa Pastorium</i> (1741), and <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XVI" title="Pope Gregory XVI">Pope Gregory XVI</a> in his letter <i><a href="/wiki/In_supremo_apostolatus" title="In supremo apostolatus">In supremo apostolatus</a></i> (1839), continued to condemn slavery.
</p><p>Thornberry (2002) asserts that <i>Inter caetera</i> was applied in the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Requirement_of_1513" title="Spanish Requirement of 1513">Spanish Requirement of 1513</a>, which was read to American Indians (who could not understand the colonisers' language) before hostilities against them began. They were given the option to accept the authority of the pope and Spanish crown or face being attacked and subjugated.<sup id="cite_ref-Thornberry_65_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thornberry_65-66">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> In 1993, the Indigenous Law Institute called on Pope John Paul II to revoke <i>Inter caetera</i> and to make reparation for "this unreasonable historical grief". This was followed by a similar appeal in 1994 by the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_World_Religions" class="mw-redirect" title="Parliament of World Religions">Parliament of World Religions</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Thornberry_65_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thornberry_65-66">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Last_years">Last years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Last years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>A danger now arose in the shape of a conspiracy by the deposed despots, the Orsini, and of some of Cesare's own <i><a href="/wiki/Condottieri" class="mw-redirect" title="Condottieri">condottieri</a></i>. At first, the papal troops were defeated and things looked bleak for the house of Borgia. But a promise of French help quickly forced the confederates to come to terms. Cesare, by an act of treachery, then seized the ringleaders at <a href="/wiki/Senigallia" title="Senigallia">Senigallia</a> and put <a href="/wiki/Oliverotto_da_Fermo" class="mw-redirect" title="Oliverotto da Fermo">Oliverotto da Fermo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Vitellozzo_Vitelli" title="Vitellozzo Vitelli">Vitellozzo Vitelli</a> to death (31 December 1502). When Alexander VI heard the news, he lured Cardinal Orsini to the Vatican and cast him into a dungeon, where he died. His goods were confiscated and many other members of the clan in Rome were arrested, while Alexander's son Goffredo Borgia led an expedition into the Campagna and seized their castles. Thus the two great houses of Orsini and Colonna, who had long fought for predominance in Rome and often flouted the pope's authority, were subjugated and the Borgias' power increased. Cesare then returned to Rome, where his father asked him to assist Goffredo in reducing the last Orsini strongholds; this he was unwilling to do, much to his father's annoyance; but he eventually marched out, captured <a href="/wiki/Ceri" title="Ceri">Ceri</a> and made peace with Giulio Orsini, who surrendered <a href="/wiki/Bracciano" title="Bracciano">Bracciano</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Villari_38-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Villari-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The war between France and Spain for the possession of Naples dragged on, and the pope was forever intriguing, ready to ally himself with whichever power promised the most advantageous terms at any moment. He offered to help Louis XII on condition that <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a> be given to Cesare, and then offered to help Spain in exchange for <a href="/wiki/Siena" title="Siena">Siena</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pisa" title="Pisa">Pisa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg/220px-Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="308" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg/330px-Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg/440px-Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg 2x" data-file-width="732" data-file-height="1024" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Vannozza_Cattanei.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Vannozza_dei_Cattanei" title="Vannozza dei Cattanei">Vannozza dei Cattanei</a></div></div></div>
<p>Of Alexander's many mistresses, one of his favourites was <a href="/wiki/Vannozza_dei_Cattanei" title="Vannozza dei Cattanei">Vannozza dei Cattanei</a>, born in 1442, and wife of three successive husbands. The connection began in 1470, and she had four children whom the pope openly acknowledged as his own: <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare</a> (born 1475), <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia,_2nd_Duke_of_Gand%C3%ADa" title="Giovanni Borgia, 2nd Duke of Gandía">Giovanni</a>, afterwards duke of <a href="/wiki/Gandia" title="Gandia">Gandia</a> (commonly known as Juan, born 1474 or 1476), <a href="/wiki/Lucrezia_Borgia" title="Lucrezia Borgia">Lucrezia</a> (born 1480), and <a href="/wiki/Gioffre_Borgia" title="Gioffre Borgia">Gioffre</a> (Goffredo in Italian, born 1481 or 1482). For a period of time, before legitimizing his children after becoming pope, Rodrigo pretended that his four children with Vannozza were his niece and nephews and that they were fathered by Vannozza's husbands.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/G._J._Meyer" title="G. J. Meyer">G. J. Meyer</a> has argued that the birth dates of the four in comparison with Alexander's known whereabouts actually preclude him having fathered any of them, and that his "acknowledgement" merely consisted of addressing them as "beloved son / daughter" in correspondence (while applying the same address to, e.g., <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">Ferdinand II of Aragon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Isabella_I_of_Castile" title="Isabella I of Castile">Isabella I of Castile</a> in the same letter).<sup id="cite_ref-Meyer_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meyer-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Another mistress was the beautiful <a href="/wiki/Giulia_Farnese" title="Giulia Farnese">Giulia Farnese</a> ("<i>Giulia la Bella</i>"), wife of <a href="/wiki/Orsino_Orsini" title="Orsino Orsini">Orsino Orsini</a>. Giulia was mother of <a href="/wiki/Laura_Orsini" title="Laura Orsini">Laura Orsini</a>, born in 1492, probably daughter of Alexander. However, he still loved Vannozza and his children by her. Caring for them proved the determining factor of his whole career. He lavished vast sums on them. Vannozza lived in the Palace of a late Cardinal, or in a large, palatial villa. The children lived between their mother's home and the Papal Palace itself.<sup id="cite_ref-Villari_38-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Villari-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
