		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 176px"><div style="width: 176px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 171px;"><div style="margin:25.5px auto;"><a href="/wiki/File:JoffreBorgia.jpg" class="image" title="Gioffre Borgia (1482–1517) Prince of Squillace."><img alt="Gioffre Borgia (1482–1517) Prince of Squillace." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/JoffreBorgia.jpg/141px-JoffreBorgia.jpg" decoding="async" width="141" height="179" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/JoffreBorgia.jpg/212px-JoffreBorgia.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/JoffreBorgia.jpg/282px-JoffreBorgia.jpg 2x" data-file-width="316" data-file-height="401" /></a></div></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
<p><a href="/wiki/Gioffre_Borgia" title="Gioffre Borgia">Gioffre Borgia</a> (1482–1517) Prince of Squillace.
</p>
			</div>
		</div></li>
</ul>
<p>Six other children, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Girolama_Borgia&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Girolama Borgia (page does not exist)">Girolama</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Isabella_Borgia&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Isabella Borgia (page does not exist)">Isabella</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pier_Luigi_de_Borgia,_1st_duke_of_Gand%C3%ADa" class="mw-redirect" title="Pier Luigi de Borgia, 1st duke of Gandía">Pedro-Luiz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia_(Infans_Romanus)" title="Giovanni Borgia (Infans Romanus)">Giovanni the "Infans Romanus"</a>, Rodrigo and Bernardo, were of uncertain maternal parentage. His daughter Isabella was the great-great-grandmother of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_X" title="Pope Innocent X">Pope Innocent X</a>, who was therefore descended in a direct line from Alexander.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>He is an ancestor of virtually all royal houses of Europe, mainly the southern and western ones, for being the ancestor of <i>Dona</i> <a href="/wiki/Luisa_de_Guzm%C3%A1n" title="Luisa de Guzmán">Luisa de Guzmán</a>, wife of King <a href="/wiki/John_IV_of_Portugal" title="John IV of Portugal">John IV of Portugal</a>, of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Braganza" title="House of Braganza">House of Braganza</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 176px"><div style="width: 176px">

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_(_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_%28_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI%29.jpg/220px-Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_%28_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="322" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_%28_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI%29.jpg/330px-Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_%28_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_%28_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI%29.jpg/440px-Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_%28_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2694" data-file-height="3944" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Tomb_of_Popes_Borja_(_Callisto_III_and_Alessandro_VI).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/List_of_extant_papal_tombs#15th_century" title="List of extant papal tombs">The tomb of Pope Alexander VI</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg/220px-Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg/330px-Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg/440px-Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2516" data-file-height="2009" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kmska_Titiaan_-_Jacopo_Pesaro_bisschop_van_Paphos_voorgesteld_door_paus_Alexander_VI_Borgia_aan_de_heilige_Petrus_-_28-02-2010_13-56-55.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i><a href="/wiki/Jacopo_Pesaro_being_presented_by_Pope_Alexander_VI_to_Saint_Peter" title="Jacopo Pesaro being presented by Pope Alexander VI to Saint Peter">Jacopo Pesaro being presented by Pope Alexander VI to Saint Peter</a></i>, painting by <a href="/wiki/Titian" title="Titian">Titian</a></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare</a> was preparing for another expedition in August 1503 when, after he and his father had dined with Cardinal Adriano Castellesi on 6 August, they were taken ill with fever a few days later. Cesare, who "lay in bed, his skin peeling and his face suffused to a violet colour"<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> as a consequence of certain drastic measures to save him, eventually recovered; but the aged Pontiff apparently had little chance. <a href="/wiki/Johann_Burchard" title="Johann Burchard">Burchard's</a> <i>Diary</i> provides a few details of the pope's final illness and death at age 72:<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>Saturday, 12 August 1503, the pope fell ill in the morning. After the hour of vespers, between six and seven o'clock a fever appeared and remained permanently. On 15 August thirteen ounces of blood were drawn from him and the <a href="/wiki/Tertian_ague" class="mw-redirect" title="Tertian ague">tertian ague</a> supervened. On Thursday, 17 August, at nine o'clock in the forenoon he took medicine. On Friday, the 18th, between nine and ten o'clock he confessed to the Bishop Gamboa of Carignola, who then read Mass to him. After his Communion he gave the Eucharist to the pope who was sitting in bed. Then he ended the Mass at which were present five cardinals, Serra, Juan and Francesco Borgia, Casanova and Loris. The pope told them that he felt very bad. At the hour of vespers after Gamboa had given him Extreme Unction, he died.</p></blockquote>
<p>As for his true faults, known only to his confessor, Pope Alexander VI apparently died genuinely repentant.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;Note 10&#93;</a></sup> The bishop of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Gallipoli" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Gallipoli">Gallipoli</a>, Alexis Celadoni, spoke of the pontiff's contrition during his funeral oration<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;Note 11&#93;</a></sup> to the electors of Alexander's successor, pope <a href="/wiki/Pius_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius III">Pius III</a>:<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;Note 12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>When at last the pope was suffering from a very severe sickness, he spontaneously requested, one after another, each of the last sacraments. He first made a very careful confession of his sins, with a contrite heart, and was affected even to the shedding of tears, I am told; then he received in Communion the most Sacred Body and Extreme Unction was administered to him.</p></blockquote>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Interregnum" title="Interregnum">interregnum</a> witnessed again the ancient "tradition" of violence and rioting.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;Note 13&#93;</a></sup> Cesare, too ill to attend to the business himself, sent <a href="/wiki/Micheletto_Corella" title="Micheletto Corella">Don Micheletto</a>, his chief <i>bravo</i>, to seize the pope's treasures before the death was publicly announced. The next day the body was exhibited to the people and clergy of Rome, but was covered by an "old tapestry" ("antiquo tapete"), having become greatly disfigured by rapid decomposition. According to <a href="/wiki/Raffaello_Maffei" title="Raffaello Maffei">Raphael Volterrano</a>: "It was a revolting scene to look at that deformed, blackened corpse, prodigiously swelled, and exhaling an infectious smell; his lips and nose were covered with brown drivel, his mouth was opened very widely, and his tongue, inflated by poison, ... therefore no fanatic or devotee dared to kiss his feet or hands, as custom would have required."<sup id="cite_ref-NC_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NC-86">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The Venetian ambassador stated that the body was "the ugliest, most monstrous and horrible dead body that was ever seen, without any form or likeness of humanity".<sup id="cite_ref-NC_86-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NC-86">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_von_Pastor" title="Ludwig von Pastor">Ludwig von Pastor</a> insists that the decomposition was "perfectly natural", owing to the summer heat.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;Note 14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Commentaries<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (June 2021)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> attribute the pope's death to <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a>, then prevalent in Rome, or to another such pestilence.<sup id="cite_ref-poison1_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-poison1-89">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> One contemporary official wrote home that there was little surprise that Alexander and Cesare had both fallen ill, as the <a href="/wiki/Miasma_theory_of_disease" class="mw-redirect" title="Miasma theory of disease">bad air</a> had led to many in Rome, and especially in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Curia" title="Roman Curia">Roman Curia</a>, becoming sick.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After a short stay, the body was removed from the crypts of St. Peter's and installed in the less well-known Spanish national church of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_in_Monserrato_degli_Spagnoli" title="Santa Maria in Monserrato degli Spagnoli">Santa Maria in Monserrato degli Spagnoli</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"/><table class="box-Too_few_opinions plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-too_few_opinions" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/40px-Ambox_important.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="40" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/60px-Ambox_important.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/80px-Ambox_important.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="40" data-file-height="40" /></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">The examples and perspective in this section <b>may not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:WikiProject_Countering_systemic_bias" title="Wikipedia:WikiProject Countering systemic bias">include all significant viewpoints</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit">improve the article</a> or <a href="/wiki/Talk:Pope_Alexander_VI" title="Talk:Pope Alexander VI">discuss the issue</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2022</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg/220px-Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="259" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg/330px-Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg/440px-Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="510" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexander_VI_-_Pinturicchio_detail.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Detail of fresco <i>Resurrection</i> in the Borgia Apartments, showing Alexander VI humbly in prayer<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Following the death of Alexander VI, his rival and successor <a href="/wiki/Julius_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Julius II">Julius II</a> said on the day of his election: "I will not live in the same rooms as the Borgias lived. He desecrated the Holy Church as none before."<sup id="cite_ref-NC2_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NC2-93">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Borgia_Apartments" title="Borgia Apartments">Borgia Apartments</a> remained sealed until the 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-NC2_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NC2-93">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catholic apologists of Alexander VI have argued that the behaviors he receives criticism for were not atypical of the period. <a href="/wiki/Joseph_de_Maistre" title="Joseph de Maistre">De Maistre</a>, in his work <i>Du Pape</i>, "The latter are forgiven nothing, because everything is expected from them, wherefore the vices lightly passed over in a <a href="/wiki/Louis_XIV_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XIV of France">Louis XIV</a> become most offensive and scandalous in an Alexander VI."<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander VI had sought reforms of the increasingly irresponsible Curia, putting together a group of his most pious cardinals in order to move the process along. The planned reforms included new rules on the sale of Church property, the limiting of cardinals to one diocese, and stricter moral codes for clergy, though these were not implemented.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;Note 15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander VI was known for his patronage of the arts, and in his days a new architectural era was initiated in Rome with the coming of <a href="/wiki/Donato_Bramante" title="Donato Bramante">Bramante</a>. <a href="/wiki/Raffaello_Santi" class="mw-redirect" title="Raffaello Santi">Raphael</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Pinturicchio" title="Pinturicchio">Pinturicchio</a> all worked for him.<sup id="cite_ref-Villari_38-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Villari-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> He commissioned Pinturicchio to lavishly paint a suite of rooms in the Apostolic Palace in the Vatican, which are today known as the <a href="/wiki/Borgia_Apartments" title="Borgia Apartments">Borgia Apartments</a>. He took a great interest in theatrics, and he even had the <a href="/wiki/Menaechmi" title="Menaechmi">Menaechmi</a> performed in his apartments.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to the arts, Alexander VI also encouraged the development of education. In 1495, he issued a <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> at the request of <a href="/wiki/William_Elphinstone" title="William Elphinstone">William Elphinstone</a>, Bishop of Aberdeen, and <a href="/wiki/James_IV_of_Scotland" title="James IV of Scotland">James IV of Scotland</a>, founding <a href="/wiki/King%27s_College,_Aberdeen" title="King&#39;s College, Aberdeen">King's College, Aberdeen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> King's College now forms an integral element of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Aberdeen" title="University of Aberdeen">University of Aberdeen</a>. Alexander VI also, in 1501, approved the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Valencia" title="University of Valencia">University of Valencia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander VI, whom papal rival and successor <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_della_Rovere" class="mw-redirect" title="Giuliano della Rovere">Giuliano della Rovere</a> alleged without evidence was a <a href="/wiki/Marrano" title="Marrano">marrano</a>, demonstrated relatively benign treatment of Jews. After the 1492 <a href="/wiki/Expulsion_of_the_Jews_from_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Expulsion of the Jews from Spain">expulsion of the Jews from Spain</a>, some 9,000 impoverished <a href="/wiki/Iberian_Jews" class="mw-redirect" title="Iberian Jews">Iberian Jews</a> arrived at the borders of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>. Alexander welcomed them into Rome, declaring that they were "permitted to lead their life, free from interference from Christians, to continue in their own rites, to gain wealth, and to enjoy many other privileges". He similarly allowed the immigration of <a href="/wiki/Expulsion_of_the_Jews_from_Portugal" class="mw-redirect" title="Expulsion of the Jews from Portugal">Jews expelled from Portugal</a> in 1497 and from Provence in 1498.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Bohuslav_Hasi%C5%A1tejnsk%C3%BD_z_Lobkovic" title="Bohuslav Hasištejnský z Lobkovic">Bohuslav Hasištejnský z Lobkovic</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Bohemia" title="Bohemia">Bohemian</a> <a href="/wiki/Humanism" title="Humanism">humanist</a> poet (1461–1510), wrote a hostile epitaph on Alexander in Latin:<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1123665981">.mw-parser-output .verse_translation .translated{padding-left:2em}@media only screen and (max-width:43.75em){.mw-parser-output .verse_translation.wrap_when_small td{display:block;padding-left:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .verse_translation.wrap_when_small .translated{padding-left:0.5em}}</style>
<table role="presentation" class="verse_translation wrap_when_small" style="margin-left:1em !important;">

<tbody><tr style="vertical-align: top;">
<td><div style="font-style:italic;text-align:left" lang="" class="poem">
<p><b>Epitaphium Alexandri Papae</b><br />
<br />
Cui tranquilla quies odio, cui proelia cordi<br />
et rixa et caedes seditioque fuit,<br />
mortuus hac recubat populis gaudentibus urna<br />
pastor Alexander, maxima Roma, tuus.<br />
Vos, Erebi proceres, vos caeli claudite portas<br />
atque Animam vestris hanc prohibete locis.<br />
In Styga nam veniens pacem turbabit Averni,<br />
committet superos, si petat astra, poli.
</p>
</div>
</td>
<td class="translated"><div class="poem">
<p>'<i>Epitaph to Pope Alexander</i><br />
<br />
One who hated peace and quiet,<br />
and who loved battles, strife, murder and treason,<br />
Lies in this urn as all peoples rejoice,<br />
Alexander, thy shepherd, o greatest Rome.<br />
Ye prelates of <a href="/wiki/Erebus" title="Erebus">Erebus</a>, close the doors of Heaven<br />
and prohibit this Soul from your realm.<br />
If it enters <a href="/wiki/Styx" title="Styx">Styx</a>, it will disrupt the peace of <a href="/wiki/Avernus" title="Avernus">Avernus</a>,<br />
and if it seeks heaven it will set the inhabitants of the sky against each other.
</p>
</div>
</td></tr>
<tr style="display: none;">
<td>
</td>
<td style="padding-left: 2em;">
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Despite Julius II's hostility, the Roman barons and Romagna vicars were never again to be the same problem for the papacy and Julius' successes owe much to the foundations laid by the Borgias.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Unlike Julius, Alexander never made war unless absolutely necessary, preferring negotiation and diplomacy.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexander Lee argues that the crimes attributed to the Borgias were exaggerated by contemporaries because they were outsiders expanding their holdings at the expense of the Italians, that they were Spaniards when it was felt that Spain had too much control on the Italian peninsula, and that after the death of Alexander the family lost its influence and therefore any incentive for anyone to defend them.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Alexander_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-5"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-5">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Claims of paternity and numbers of children are disputed. See G. J. Meyer,<sup id="cite_ref-Meyer_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meyer-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Christopher Hibbert,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and Ferdinand Gregorovius.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> There are claims about Cesare, Giovanni, Lucrezia, Gioffre (or Goffredo), Girolama (or Jeronima), Isabella, <a href="/wiki/Pier_Luigi_Borgia" class="mw-redirect" title="Pier Luigi Borgia">Pier Luigi</a> (or Pedro Luis), Bernardo, Rodrigo, <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Borgia_(Infans_Romanus)" title="Giovanni Borgia (Infans Romanus)">Giovanni Infans Romanus</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Laura_Orsini" title="Laura Orsini">Laura</a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-6"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-6">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Alessandro VI</i>, <a href="/wiki/Valencian_language" title="Valencian language">Valencian</a>: <i lang="ca-valencia">Alexandre VI</i>, <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Alejandro VI</i></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-7"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-7">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Valencian_language" title="Valencian language">Valencian</a>: <i lang="ca-valencia">Roderic Llançol i de Borja</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ca-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Catalan" title="Help:IPA/Catalan">[roðeˈɾiɡ ʎanˈsɔl i ðe ˈbɔɾdʒa]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>: <i lang="es">Rodrigo Lanzol y de Borja</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="es-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Spanish" title="Help:IPA/Spanish">[roˈðɾiɣo lanˈθol i ðe ˈβoɾxa]</a></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-47"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-47">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"We need now to digress a little to explain why the pope should bestow his favours so generously on his own relatives. Let us take a look first at the political situation in Spain and in Italy. For centuries, Spain had been almost completely overridden by the Moors. The Spaniards had been trying to take back their country from the Moors for almost 800 years. By the middle of the 15th century, this reconquest was almost complete, but Spain was still a hodgepodge of competing principalities and, because of its constant state of warfare, still a very backward country. In Italy, on the other hand, the Renaissance, which had hardly begun in Spain, had reached its high point and the Italians, in general, did not look kindly on a citizen of this backward country being elevated to the highest post in the Church. Remember, too, that the pope at the time, besides his spiritual powers, was a sovereign political power with large areas of the peninsula, nominally, at least, under his control. Italy was, politically, in a worse state than Spain. In the south, Naples was a fief of the pope, but its ruler, King Ferrante, refused to acknowledge the pope's authority. In the north of the peninsula, many small principalities vied for dominance and were often at war with one another, changing alliances as rapidly as opportunity invited. In the Papal States themselves, noble families, such as the Orsini and the Colonna, acted as petty tyrants in the cities and areas which they controlled, grinding down the people and constantly seeking to achieve their independence from their sovereign, the pope. These Roman families even sought to control the Papacy itself. It was probably only because they could not agree on an Italian successor to Nicholas V that the elderly Callistus had been elected; one who, in all probability, would not live long. (Remember that, in our own times, John XXIII was supposed to have been elected for the same reason). Callistus III was acknowledged by all as religious and austere, though severely criticized for his largesse to his family. But he was surrounded by enemies both within the Church and among the rulers of Europe. When elected, he did what all leaders do, he surrounded himself with people whom he believed he could trust. A Spaniard in Italy, he was hard-pressed to find such trustworthiness except from members of his own family; hence his patronage of them, though it is not to be denied that it was probably also for personal reasons."<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-49"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-49">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"As for her reputation, there is absolutely no evidence for the rumors of incest with one or more of her brothers – or indeed with her father – apart from that given by her first husband, Giovanni Sforza, during the divorce proceedings, during which several other baseless accusations were leveled in both directions."<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-53"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-53">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"The holy year 1500 definitively ushered in the custom of opening a holy door on Christmas Eve and closing it the following year on Christmas Day. Alexander opened the first holy door in St. Peter's Basilica on Christmas Eve, 1499, and papal legates opened the doors in the other three patriarchal basilicas. For this occasion, Pope Alexander had a new opening created in the portico of St. Peter's and commissioned a door, made of marble, 3.5 meters high and 2.2 wide. It lasted until 1618 when another door was installed in the new basilica. The door, in turn, was replaced in 1950 by the bronze door, which is still in use. In a ceremony strikingly similar in many ways to today's ritual opening of a holy door, Alexander was carried in the gestatorial chair to the portico of St. Peter's. He and the members of his retinue, bearing long candles, processed to the holy door, as the choir intoned Psalm 118:19–20: "Open for me the gate of Yahweh, where the upright go in." The pope knocked thrice on the door, it gave way (assisted from within by workers), and everyone then crossed the threshold to enter into a period of penance and reconciliation. Thus, Pope Alexander, a lover of pomp and ceremony, formalized the rite of opening a holy door and began a tradition that continues, with few variations, to this day. Similar rites were held at the other patriarchal basilicas. Alexander was also the first to institute a special rite for the closing of a holy door. On the feast of the Epiphany, 6 January 1501, two cardinals – one with a silver brick and the other with a gold one – symbolically began to seal the holy door. Basilica workers known as sanpietrini completed the task, which included placing small coins and medals, minted during the holy year, inside the wall."<sup id="cite_ref-holydoors_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-holydoors-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-55"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-55">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"[A]ll the clergy of the city were invited to the opening of the [1500] Jubilee. The pope himself performed this ceremony on Christmas Eve, 1499, having taken pains to settle all the details beforehand with his Master of Ceremonies. The ceremonial observed on these occasions was no modern invention, but, as the Bull of Indiction expressly says, was founded on ancient rites and full of symbolic meaning. According to Burchard, the crowd which assisted at these solemnities numbered 200,000 persons. Although this may be an exaggeration, still it is certain that, in spite of the troubles of the times and the insecurity in Rome itself, the numbers attending this Jubilee were very large."<sup id="cite_ref-rorate-jubilee_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rorate-jubilee-54">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-58"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-58">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"The permanent group of skilled workers and artisans, in every trade, who with their assistants take care of St. Peter's Basilica."<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-71"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-71">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Doubtful, but possible. On a similar claim: "Without any solid evidence Giulia is said to have been the model for Pinturicchio's 'Virgin and Child' surrounded by angels in the Borgia Apartments of the Vatican."<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-75"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-75">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"[T]here is every reason to believe," writes the <i><a href="/wiki/Dublin_Review_(Catholic_periodical)" title="Dublin Review (Catholic periodical)">Dublin Review</a>,</i> that Pope Alexander VI died "in sentiments of piety and devotion."<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-78"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-78">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The historical value of Bishop Celadoni's funeral oration is said to be immense: "On 16 Sept 1503 Burchardus records in his diary that Alexius Celadenus or Celadonius, bishop of Gallipoli, delivered a discourse to the cardinals about to enter into conclave for the election of a successor to Pope Alexander VI. <i>Et fuit tediosa et longa oratio</i>. Burchardus's most recent editor, Thuasne, states that this oration exists in manuscript in the Bibliothèque Nationale&#160;... He omits to observe that, granting that the discourse may have been too long for the cardinals, the longer the better for us, inasmuch as it <i>contains an account of Pope Alexander of almost unique value,</i> not merely as the judgment of a contemporary, but as delivered in public before an audience of contemporaries whose station in the church had brought them into almost daily intercourse with the deceased pope, and before whom <i>any serious misrepresentation would have been impossible</i>&#160;..."<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-81"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-81">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Latin text: "Dum graviter aegrotaret, factorum conscientia punctus contrito dolentique animo ad lachrymas ut audio fusus, sacrosanctum communionis corpus sua sponte, dilutis prius diligentissima confessione peccatis, petierit, et alia sacramenta&#160;..."<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-85"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-85">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"Throughout the Middle Ages a 'tradition' or 'custom' involving pillaging was attached to the death and election of high-ranking prelates."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> And as early as 633, "the <a href="/wiki/Fourth_Council_of_Toledo" title="Fourth Council of Toledo">Fourth Council of Toledo</a> condemned the violence of the <a href="/wiki/Interregnum" title="Interregnum">interregnum</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Nor were the Romans alone guilty of such misbehavior. In the eleventh century, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Damian" title="Peter Damian">Peter Damian</a>, writing to the clergy and people of <a href="/wiki/Osimo" title="Osimo">Osimo</a>, sharply reproved the "perverse and wholly detestable practice of certain people, who at the death of the bishop break in like enemies and rob his house, like thieves make off with his belongings, set fire to the homes on his estate, and with fierce and savage barbarity cut down his grape vines and orchards".<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-88"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-88">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"In consequence of the simultaneous illness of both the Pope and his son, and the rapid decomposition of the body, which, considering the heat of the weather, was perfectly natural, the cry of poison was raised at once; but on 19 August the Mantuan Envoy writes that there was no sort of ground for supposing this."<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-96"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-96">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"As part of his proposed new reforms, Alexander now nominated a commission of six of the most pious cardinals, and less than two months later a draft Bull of Reformation had been prepared. The pope was banned from selling benefices and from transferring Church property to laypersons. As for the cardinals, who were to be drawn from all the nations, none should possess more than one bishopric; their households were limited to eighty people and thirty horses; they were banned from hunting, theaters, carnivals, and tournaments; and their funeral expenses were not to exceed 1,500 ducats. The lesser clergy were similarly reined in: they must refuse all bribes and put away their concubines."<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<li><a href="/wiki/History_of_the_papacy_(1048%E2%80%931257)" title="History of the papacy (1048–1257)">Conflicts with the HRE</a> (1048–1257)</li>
<li>Wandering Papacy
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Viterbo_Papacy" title="Viterbo Papacy">Viterbo</a>, 1257–1281</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Orvieto_Papacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Orvieto Papacy">Orvieto</a>, 1262–1297</li>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="navbox-group" style="background-color:gold;width:1%">Early Modern and<br /> Modern Era</th><td class="navbox-list-with-group navbox-list navbox-even hlist" style="width:100%;padding:0"><div style="padding:0 0.25em">
